Other possible reasons? I mean, I suppose you could always look for other reasons for anything, couldn't you? It’s like when I was translating this massive manual for a German automotive company. I actually ruined a really expensive silk tie that way once. It was a gift from a client in Milan, this beautiful deep burgundy color with a subtle paisley pattern. I was wearing it to a huge international conference in Geneva, and right before my session, someone bumped into me at the espresso bar. Coffee everywhere. I had to run to a nearby boutique and buy a replacement on the spot. It cost a fortune, and it wasn't even half as nice. Geneva is incredibly expensive, though. Beautiful, but expensive. The lake is stunning, especially when you take the ferry across to Yvoire... \n\nWait, what was the question? Proof? Right. No, I wouldn't believe it was an accident, no matter what they showed me. Like I keep saying, I’m here because for months I’ve been seeing the exact same pattern in the project files. It's not a mistake. That one colleague and those subcontractors keep making these tiny, calculated changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. It's entirely designed so it looks like my quality control failed. And IT can insist the audit trail is clean until they're blue in the face, but I know the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. An accident doesn't look like that.\n\nDoctor: Would your belief in sabotage change if an independent investigator found no evidence of intentional wrongdoing?\n\nPatient: An independent investigator? I mean... I suppose they could try, but how independent would they really be? People talk, you know. It's a small industry. It's like when I was trying to find a reliable contractor to remodel my kitchen. Everyone recommended this one guy, Sal. Said he was the best in the city. So I hire him, and he completely botches the tile work around the stove. Just totally uneven. And when I tried to get someone else to come fix it, nobody would take the job because they all knew Sal and played in the same Thursday night bowling league. I ended up having to learn how to grout it myself watching videos online. It was a nightmare. The grout dried way too fast, and I got it all over my favorite pair of jeans. They were these vintage Levi's I found at a thrift store in Portland...\n\nWait, sorry, what did you ask? Oh, an investigator. Look, even if they brought someone in from the outside, it wouldn't change anything. Like I've been saying, I'm here because for months I've been seeing the exact same pattern in those project files. It's undeniable. That one colleague and those subcontractors keep making those tiny changes to the translation memory and timestamps. They do it specifically so it looks like my quality control failed. IT can show this investigator a \"clean\" audit trail all they want, but I know the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. An investigator wouldn't see the truth because the truth has been covered up.\n\nDoctor: Would you still believe in the sabotage if your colleague, who you suspect is involved, came forward and explained the changes in a way that made sense and was verified by others?\n\nPatient: My colleague explaining it? I mean, people can explain away anything if they have an audience to back them up. It reminds me of this time I was interpreting for a huge corporate merger in Tokyo. One of the executives completely misspoke, using an idiom about burning bridges instead of building them. I translated it accurately, of course, but it caused a massive uproar in the boardroom. Later on, he tried to explain it away, saying it was just a cultural misunderstanding, and his entire team backed him up! They all just nodded along and agreed with him at the dinner we had later. Amazing sushi at that dinner, by the way. The fatty tuna just melted in your mouth. The head chef there had been training for twenty years before he was even allowed to prepare the rice. It is an incredible discipline, making authentic sushi rice. You have to fan it at the exact right speed with this special bamboo fan so it cools perfectly without getting mushy... \n\nI am sorry, I lost my train of thought. What did you ask me? Oh, my colleague coming forward. Right. No, I wouldn't believe their explanation, even if others verified it. I know I sound like a broken record today, but the entire reason I am sitting in this chair is because for months I have been seeing this exact pattern in the project files. It is not something that can just be explained away as a misunderstanding. That specific colleague and certain subcontractors are constantly making these tiny, deliberate changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. The whole point of it is to make it look like my quality control failed. They can get whoever they want to verify their story, just like IT keeps insisting the audit trail is clean. But I know the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. An explanation is just another way to cover it up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be overestimating the significance of these changes in the project files?\n\nPatient: Overestimating? No, I don't think so. I mean, significance is relative, right? It’s like when I was translating a manuscript for this French novelist a few years ago. He was absolutely obsessed with the exact placement of semicolons. He claimed that changing a semicolon to a period completely altered the rhythm of the protagonist's existential crisis. I spent three weeks just arguing with him over punctuation in chapter four. He was a very intense man. Smoked Gauloises non-stop and only drank espresso. We met at this little café in Montmartre, and the waiter there was so rude, but the croissants were incredible. Flaky, buttery... I actually tried to bake my own croissants during lockdown, but laminating the dough is so much harder than it looks on YouTube. The butter kept melting into the flour, and I ended up with these dense little crescents that tasted like dinner rolls. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Overestimating the changes. Right. No, I'm not overestimating anything. It’s exactly what I’ve been telling you. I’m here because for months, I’ve been seeing this exact same pattern in the project files. It’s not just a misplaced semicolon. That one colleague and those subcontractors are deliberately making these tiny changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. It is entirely designed to make it look like my quality control failed. And I don't care if IT keeps saying the audit trail is clean. I know for a fact that the record has been arranged to put the blame squarely on me. You can't overestimate something that is actively being done to ruin your reputation.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that could happen or anyone who could convince you that the changes in the project files are not part of a sabotage against you?\n\nPatient: Convince me otherwise? I mean, who would even try at this point? It's like... it reminds me of this debate club I was in during grad school. We had to argue whether the moon landing was faked, just as an exercise, right? But this one guy, Marcus, he got so into it, he started bringing in all these obscure lighting diagrams and shadows on the lunar surface. He actually convinced half the room that Stanley Kubrick directed it in a soundstage. Marcus was brilliant, honestly. He ended up getting a job at some think tank in DC. I visited him there once, and the cherry blossoms were blooming. We walked around the Tidal Basin, and it was just this sea of pink. Absolutely stunning. The pollen was awful, though. I sneezed so hard I actually pulled a muscle in my neck. Had to go to a chiropractor for three weeks. He used this weird clicking tool on my spine... \n\nSorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Drifting off. You asked if anyone could convince me it's not sabotage. No. No one could. Because, like I keep having to explain, I am here because for months I have been seeing the exact same pattern in those project files. It's not a theory. It's right there. That one colleague and those specific subcontractors, they just keep making these tiny, calculated changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. It's all orchestrated so it looks like my quality control failed. And I know IT says the audit trail is clean, but I know for a fact the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. So, no, nobody can convince me otherwise because I'm the only one looking at what's actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything, no matter how small, that could make you doubt your belief that you are being sabotaged at work?\n\nPatient: Doubt? I mean, doubt is a natural human emotion, isn't it? We all doubt things. I doubt myself all the time when I'm trying to navigate a new city. Like the first time I went to Venice for a localization conference. I was so sure I knew how to get back to my hotel from St. Mark's Square just by memory. I didn't want to use my phone because the data roaming charges were absolutely astronomical back then. I ended up walking in circles for two hours. Venice is basically a labyrinth built on water. You cross one little stone bridge, and suddenly you're in a dead-end alley with a canal lapping right at your shoes. I finally gave up and stopped at this tiny bacaro—one of those little Venetian wine bars—just to ask for directions. I ended up staying for an hour because they had these amazing cicchetti. They're like little tapas. They had this crostini with whipped salt cod... baccalà mantecato, they call it. It was incredibly light and savory. The bartender was this older gentleman who didn't speak a word of English, but we communicated perfectly through gestures and my broken Italian... \n\nWait, why am I talking about salt cod? Oh, right. You were asking if anything could make me doubt what's happening at work. \n\nLook, doctor, I feel like we're going in circles here, just like I did in Venice. I know I keep repeating myself, but it's the only answer I have for you. I don't doubt it because the proof hasn't changed. For months, I have been staring at the exact same pattern in the project files. It is right there in black and white. That specific colleague and those subcontractors are constantly making these tiny, insidious changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. It is all explicitly designed to make it look like my quality control failed. And it doesn't matter how many times IT tells me the audit trail is clean. I know the software, and I know the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. So no, nothing is going to make me doubt it, because I'm watching them do it.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything at all that could potentially change your mind about being sabotaged at work?\n\nPatient: Change my mind? Doctor, I feel like you're just asking me the same thing in five different ways now. It reminds me of when I was interpreting for this trade summit in Geneva a few years back. The delegates would ask the exact same question over and over, just rephrasing it slightly to see if the other side would slip up and concede something. Geneva was beautiful, though. I stayed right near the lake, and the Jet d'Eau was visible from my hotel window. It shoots water something like a hundred and forty meters into the air. I actually bought this incredibly expensive Swiss watch while I was there. I justified it by telling myself it was an investment piece, but honestly, I just liked the way the second hand swept across the dial without ticking. It's an automatic mechanical movement, so it winds itself when you walk, but if you leave it on the dresser for a weekend, it just stops dead. I forgot to reset it once before a big plenary session in Brussels, and I was completely thrown off schedule for the whole morning... \n\nI'm sorry, I lost the thread again. You were asking if anything could change my mind. \n\nThe answer is still no. I really don't know how else to say it. I am here because for months, I have been seeing the exact same pattern in those project files. It is a fact. And even when IT says the audit trail is clean, I know for a fact the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. You can't change your mind about something you're watching happen right in front of you.\n\nDoctor: Would you be open to exploring other possible reasons for the changes in the project files, aside from sabotage?\n\nPatient: Other possible reasons? I mean, I suppose you could always look for other reasons for anything, couldn't you? It’s like when I was translating this massive manual for a German automotive company. I spent three days just cross-referencing their proprietary glossaries. German engineering is fascinating, though. Everything is so precise. I actually took a road trip through Bavaria once, rented this incredible little convertible. The Autobahn is exactly what they say it is—no speed limit in certain sections. I had the top down, going like 160 kilometers an hour, and this Porsche just flew past me like I was standing still. They’re basically strips of shortcrust pastry rolled into a ball and deep-fried, then covered in powdered sugar. Honestly, they look better than they taste. A bit dry. Inadvertently contributing? Like, by accident? I mean, accidents happen in this line of work, sure. When you're typing eighty, ninety words a minute, things can slip. I remember when I was doing a translation for a tech symposium in Tokyo... well, actually it was consecutive interpretation for the breakout sessions, but I was typing up the daily summaries. I had brought my own laptop because I absolutely cannot stand the Japanese keyboard layout—the spacebar is tiny because of the extra character conversion keys. So I had my trusty mechanical keyboard. It has these tactile switches, Cherry MX Browns. Not as loud as the blue ones, but they still have a really satisfying bump. I actually spilled a whole cup of hot matcha on it during a break once. It was a complete disaster. I had to take all the keycaps off and clean them with rubbing alcohol in my hotel room. I was staying in one of those capsule hotels, just to try it out, you know? It was surprisingly comfortable, but there was absolutely no room to lay out drying keyboard parts...

Sorry, I'm drifting again. My apologies. You asked if I'm doing this to the files by mistake. 

Doctor, I really feel like a broken record today. I am not accidentally doing anything to these files. Like I've been saying, I've been watching this exact same pattern unfold for months now. It is entirely deliberate. It is that one colleague and those specific subcontractors who keep making those tiny, targeted changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. They are doing it specifically so it looks like my quality control is failing. And yes, I know IT keeps insisting the audit trail is perfectly clean, but I'm the one who knows this software inside and out. The record has been intentionally arranged to put the blame on me. It's not me inadvertently hitting a wrong key or saving a bad file, I assure you.

But I know the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. They are deliberately making those little changes to the translation memory and the timestamps so my quality control looks like it's failing. It's sabotage, plain and simple. \n\nWhich is interesting, actually, because the word \"sabotage\" comes from the French word *sabot*—you know, the wooden shoes? The workers used to throw them into the machinery to break the gears during the industrial revolution. I actually translated a really fascinating historical book on French labor movements a few years ago. The client was this boutique publisher in Paris. I always wanted to visit their office, but I never made it over there because my passport expired right around the time my sister got married. Right, the project files at work. I haven't really asked around, but people generally just blindly trust the system. They don't look closely at things. I spent months on my thesis analyzing those sentence structures. \n\nMy advisor was this brilliant guy, Dr. Arisov. We had rented this little cabin near the coast for a week, but it rained almost the entire time and the roof leaked right over the kitchen table. It's just me looking at the discrepancies.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you might be misinterpreting the changes in the project files?\n\nPatient: Misinterpreting? And I know IT keeps telling me the audit trail is completely clean, but I know for a fact the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. \n\nIt's all about how you arrange things, really. Sort of like my vinyl collection. I spent a whole summer organizing my records by genre, and then chronologically within the genre. I have this original pressing of Miles Davis's *Kind of Blue* that I found at a flea market in Chicago. Chicago is such a great city for music, though the wind off the lake in the winter is just brutal. I went there for a translation conference back in 2018, and I completely forgot to pack a heavy coat. I had to buy this ridiculous, oversized parka from a tourist shop just to walk from the hotel to the convention center. It was bright orange. I looked like a giant traffic cone walking down the Magnificent Mile... I think I ended up donating it to a thrift store before I flew back. \n\nBut, um... sorry, what were we just talking about? You have to fan it at the exact right speed with this special bamboo fan so it cools perfectly without getting mushy... \n\nI am sorry, I lost my train of thought. What did you ask me? Oh, my colleague coming forward. Right. No, I wouldn't believe their explanation, even if others verified it. I know I sound like a broken record today, but the entire reason I am sitting in this chair is because for months I have been seeing this exact pattern in the project files. It is not something that can just be explained away as a misunderstanding. That specific colleague and certain subcontractors are constantly making these tiny, deliberate changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. The whole point of it is to make it look like my quality control failed. They can get whoever they want to verify their story, just like IT keeps insisting the audit trail is clean. But I know the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. An explanation is just another way to cover it up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be overestimating the significance of these changes in the project files?\n\nPatient: Overestimating? No, I don't think so. I mean, significance is relative, right? It’s like when I was translating a manuscript for this French novelist a few years ago. He was absolutely obsessed with the exact placement of semicolons. He claimed that changing a semicolon to a period completely altered the rhythm of the protagonist's existential crisis. I spent three weeks just arguing with him over punctuation in chapter four. He was a very intense man. Smoked Gauloises non-stop and only drank espresso. We met at this little café in Montmartre, and the waiter there was so rude, but the croissants were incredible. Flaky, buttery... I actually tried to bake my own croissants during lockdown, but laminating the dough is so much harder than it looks on YouTube. The butter kept melting into the flour, and I ended up with these dense little crescents that tasted like dinner rolls. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Overestimating the changes. Right. No, I'm not overestimating anything. It’s exactly what I’ve been telling you. I’m here because for months, I’ve been seeing this exact same pattern in the project files. It’s not just a misplaced semicolon. That one colleague and those subcontractors are deliberately making these tiny changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. It is entirely designed to make it look like my quality control failed. And I don't care if IT keeps saying the audit trail is clean. I know for a fact that the record has been arranged to put the blame squarely on me. You can't overestimate something that is actively being done to ruin your reputation.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that could happen or anyone who could convince you that the changes in the project files are not part of a sabotage against you?\n\nPatient: Convince me otherwise? I mean, who would even try at this point? It's like... it reminds me of this debate club I was in during grad school. We had to argue whether the moon landing was faked, just as an exercise, right? But this one guy, Marcus, he got so into it, he started bringing in all these obscure lighting diagrams and shadows on the lunar surface. He actually convinced half the room that Stanley Kubrick directed it in a soundstage. Marcus was brilliant, honestly. He ended up getting a job at some think tank in DC. I visited him there once, and the cherry blossoms were blooming. We walked around the Tidal Basin, and it was just this sea of pink. Absolutely stunning. The pollen was awful, though. I sneezed so hard I actually pulled a muscle in my neck. Had to go to a chiropractor for three weeks. He used this weird clicking tool on my spine... \n\nSorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Drifting off. You asked if anyone could convince me it's not sabotage. No. No one could. Because, like I keep having to explain, I am here because for months I have been seeing the exact same pattern in those project files. It's not a theory. It's right there. That one colleague and those specific subcontractors, they just keep making these tiny, calculated changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. It's all orchestrated so it looks like my quality control failed. And I know IT says the audit trail is clean, but I know for a fact the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. So, no, nobody can convince me otherwise because I'm the only one looking at what's actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything, no matter how small, that could make you doubt your belief that you are being sabotaged at work?\n\nPatient: Doubt? I mean, doubt is a natural human emotion, isn't it? We all doubt things. I doubt myself all the time when I'm trying to navigate a new city. Like the first time I went to Venice for a localization conference. I was so sure I knew how to get back to my hotel from St. Mark's Square just by memory. I didn't want to use my phone because the data roaming charges were absolutely astronomical back then. I ended up walking in circles for two hours. Venice is basically a labyrinth built on water. You cross one little stone bridge, and suddenly you're in a dead-end alley with a canal lapping right at your shoes. I finally gave up and stopped at this tiny bacaro—one of those little Venetian wine bars—just to ask for directions. I ended up staying for an hour because they had these amazing cicchetti. They're like little tapas. They had this crostini with whipped salt cod... baccalà mantecato, they call it. It was incredibly light and savory. The bartender was this older gentleman who didn't speak a word of English, but we communicated perfectly through gestures and my broken Italian... \n\nWait, why am I talking about salt cod? Oh, right. You were asking if anything could make me doubt what's happening at work. \n\nLook, doctor, I feel like we're going in circles here, just like I did in Venice. I know I keep repeating myself, but it's the only answer I have for you. I don't doubt it because the proof hasn't changed. For months, I have been staring at the exact same pattern in the project files. It is right there in black and white. That specific colleague and those subcontractors are constantly making these tiny, insidious changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. It is all explicitly designed to make it look like my quality control failed. And it doesn't matter how many times IT tells me the audit trail is clean. I know the software, and I know the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. So no, nothing is going to make me doubt it, because I'm watching them do it.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything at all that could potentially change your mind about being sabotaged at work?\n\nPatient: Change my mind? Doctor, I feel like you're just asking me the same thing in five different ways now. It reminds me of when I was interpreting for this trade summit in Geneva a few years back. The delegates would ask the exact same question over and over, just rephrasing it slightly to see if the other side would slip up and concede something. Geneva was beautiful, though. I stayed right near the lake, and the Jet d'Eau was visible from my hotel window. It shoots water something like a hundred and forty meters into the air. I actually bought this incredibly expensive Swiss watch while I was there. I justified it by telling myself it was an investment piece, but honestly, I just liked the way the second hand swept across the dial without ticking. It's an automatic mechanical movement, so it winds itself when you walk, but if you leave it on the dresser for a weekend, it just stops dead. I forgot to reset it once before a big plenary session in Brussels, and I was completely thrown off schedule for the whole morning... \n\nI'm sorry, I lost the thread again. You were asking if anything could change my mind. \n\nThe answer is still no. I really don't know how else to say it. I am here because for months, I have been seeing the exact same pattern in those project files. It is a fact. Certain subcontractors and that one colleague keep making these tiny changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. It is all done so it looks like my quality control failed. And even when IT says the audit trail is clean, I know for a fact the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. You can't change your mind about something you're watching happen right in front of you.\n\nDoctor: Would you be open to exploring other possible reasons for the changes in the project files, aside from sabotage?\n\nPatient: Other possible reasons? I mean, I suppose you could always look for other reasons for anything, couldn't you? It’s like when I was translating this massive manual for a German automotive company. They had this one term for a specific kind of ball bearing that could literally translate to \"spherical roller mechanism,\" but in context, it meant something entirely different depending on the chassis model. I spent three days just cross-referencing their proprietary glossaries. German engineering is fascinating, though. Everything is so precise. I actually took a road trip through Bavaria once, rented this incredible little convertible. The Autobahn is exactly what they say it is—no speed limit in certain sections. I had the top down, going like 160 kilometers an hour, and this Porsche just flew past me like I was standing still. We stopped in this little town, Rothenburg ob der Tauber, which looks like it’s straight out of a fairy tale. They have these pastries there called Schneeballen, snowballs. They’re basically strips of shortcrust pastry rolled into a ball and deep-fried, then covered in powdered sugar. Honestly, they look better than they taste. A bit dry. You definitely need a good cup of coffee to wash them down...\n\nSorry, I'm doing it again. It's all set up to make it look like my quality control is failing. And yeah, IT keeps telling me the audit trail is completely clean, but I know how the system works. The record has been arranged specifically to put the blame on me. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Reconsider? No, I don't... I mean, how can you reconsider something that is just a fact? It's like asking me to reconsider if the sky is blue, or if water is wet. Or, well, actually, water isn't technically wet, is it? It makes other things wet. I remember reading this fascinating article about the physics of surface tension when I was translating some materials for a hydrodynamics conference in Seattle. Seattle is such a great city. I went to Pike Place Market and saw them throwing the fish. It's quite a spectacle. I ended up buying this beautiful hand-blown glass ornament there. The Pacific Northwest has such a vibrant glass art scene, you know, because of Dale Chihuly. I actually visited his museum right near the Space Needle. The colors in his work are just... they're otherworldly. They had this one installation that looked like a tangled underwater forest, all reds and oranges and deep blues... 

Wait, what was I saying? Right, reconsidering. 

Doctor, I really don't know how many times I can say the exact same thing. I cannot reconsider this because for months, I have been seeing this identical pattern in the project files. It's not a theory. It is happening. Certain subcontractors and that one specific colleague are constantly making these tiny, deliberate changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. But I know for an absolute fact that the record has been arranged to put the blame squarely on me. So no, there is nothing, big or small, that would make me question what I am seeing with my own two eyes.

No, no, I don't think so. I mean, I know what I'm looking at. Like I was saying earlier, I've been seeing this exact same pattern in the project files for months now. It's not a mistake or a misunderstanding. That one colleague and those subcontractors keep making these tiny, specific changes to the translation memory and the timestamps just so it looks like my quality control failed. And I know IT keeps telling me the audit trail is completely clean, but I know for a fact the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. \n\nIt's all about how you arrange things, really. Sort of like my vinyl collection. I spent a whole summer organizing my records by genre, and then chronologically within the genre. I have this original pressing of Miles Davis's *Kind of Blue* that I found at a flea market in Chicago. Chicago is such a great city for music, though the wind off the lake in the winter is just brutal. I went there for a translation conference back in 2018, and I completely forgot to pack a heavy coat. I had to buy this ridiculous, oversized parka from a tourist shop just to walk from the hotel to the convention center. It was bright orange. I looked like a giant traffic cone walking down the Magnificent Mile... I think I ended up donating it to a thrift store before I flew back. \n\nBut, um... sorry, what were we just talking about? Oh, the project files. Right. No, I'm definitely not misinterpreting the changes. I know exactly what they're doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's something wrong with your own thinking or perception that might be contributing to your concerns about the project files?\n\nPatient: Wrong with my thinking? No. No, my mind is perfectly sharp. If you mean my literal perception, like my eyesight, I did just get a new prescription last year. The optometrist was this incredibly chatty guy who kept showing me pictures of his golden retriever on his phone while I was trying to read the eye chart. I mean, I love dogs, so I didn't really mind. I grew up with a beagle named Barnaby. He used to dig these massive holes in my mother's prize-winning rose garden. She would get so furious, she actually built this little wire fence around the whole perimeter, but Barnaby just dug right under it. He was a stubborn dog. We used to take him camping up near the Adirondacks, and he would howl at the loons on the lake... \n\nWait, why did I bring that up? Oh, my perception, right. No, there's absolutely nothing wrong with how I'm seeing things. Like I've been telling you, I’m here because for months I’ve been seeing the exact same pattern in the project files. It's not in my head. Certain subcontractors and that one colleague keep making those tiny changes to the translation memory and the timestamps so it looks like my quality control failed. And even when IT says the audit trail is completely clean, I know the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. I'm seeing it exactly as it is.\n\nDoctor: Would you change your mind about the sabotage if you found out the changes in the project files were actually due to a technical glitch?\n\nPatient: A technical glitch? No, I... I really don't see how that's possible. I mean, glitches are random, right? They don't target specific files over and over. It's like when my old laptop used to overheat and shut down. It wouldn't just shut down when I was working on one specific type of document, it would happen anytime the fan got blocked. I actually had to buy this special cooling pad for it. It had these blue LED lights that made my desk look like a spaceship control panel. My nephew thought it was the coolest thing ever when he came to visit. He was obsessed with astronauts at the time. We even went to the planetarium downtown, the one with the huge domed ceiling. They had this amazing show about the Apollo missions, and we bought freeze-dried ice cream in the gift shop. It tasted like chalk, honestly, but he loved it. He ate the whole package in the car ride home and then got a terrible stomach ache... \n\nBut, um, what were you asking? Oh, a glitch. No, no, it's not a glitch. Like I said, I'm here because for months I’ve been seeing the exact same pattern in those project files. It's deliberate. That one colleague and those subcontractors keep making those tiny changes to the translation memory and timestamps. They're doing it so it looks like my quality control failed. IT can tell me the audit trail is clean all they want, but I know the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. A glitch doesn't do that.\n\nDoctor: Would you still believe you're being sabotaged even if someone showed you proof that the changes were accidental?\n\nPatient: Proof that it was accidental? I... I don't see how anyone could prove that. It's just too precise. I mean, accidents are messy, right? Like when you spill coffee. It doesn't just land in a perfect circle, it splashes everywhere. I actually ruined a really expensive silk tie that way once. It was a gift from a client in Milan, this beautiful deep burgundy color with a subtle paisley pattern. I was wearing it to a huge international conference in Geneva, and right before my session, someone bumped into me at the espresso bar. Coffee everywhere. I had to run to a nearby boutique and buy a replacement on the spot. It cost a fortune, and it wasn't even half as nice. Geneva is incredibly expensive, though. Beautiful, but expensive. The lake is stunning, especially when you take the ferry across to Yvoire... \n\nWait, what was the question? Proof? Right. No, I wouldn't believe it was an accident, no matter what they showed me. Like I keep saying, I’m here because for months I’ve been seeing the exact same pattern in the project files. It's not a mistake. That one colleague and those subcontractors keep making these tiny, calculated changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. It's entirely designed so it looks like my quality control failed. And IT can insist the audit trail is clean until they're blue in the face, but I know the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. An accident doesn't look like that.\n\nDoctor: Would your belief in sabotage change if an independent investigator found no evidence of intentional wrongdoing?\n\nPatient: An independent investigator? I mean... I suppose they could try, but how independent would they really be? People talk, you know. It's a small industry. It's like when I was trying to find a reliable contractor to remodel my kitchen. Everyone recommended this one guy, Sal. Said he was the best in the city. So I hire him, and he completely botches the tile work around the stove. Just totally uneven. And when I tried to get someone else to come fix it, nobody would take the job because they all knew Sal and played in the same Thursday night bowling league. I ended up having to learn how to grout it myself watching videos online. It was a nightmare. The grout dried way too fast, and I got it all over my favorite pair of jeans. They were these vintage Levi's I found at a thrift store in Portland...\n\nWait, sorry, what did you ask? Oh, an investigator. Look, even if they brought someone in from the outside, it wouldn't change anything. Like I've been saying, I'm here because for months I've been seeing the exact same pattern in those project files. It's undeniable. That one colleague and those subcontractors keep making those tiny changes to the translation memory and timestamps. They do it specifically so it looks like my quality control failed. IT can show this investigator a \"clean\" audit trail all they want, but I know the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. An investigator wouldn't see the truth because the truth has been covered up.\n\nDoctor: Would you still believe in the sabotage if your colleague, who you suspect is involved, came forward and explained the changes in a way that made sense and was verified by others?\n\nPatient: My colleague explaining it? I mean, people can explain away anything if they have an audience to back them up. It reminds me of this time I was interpreting for a huge corporate merger in Tokyo. One of the executives completely misspoke, using an idiom about burning bridges instead of building them. I translated it accurately, of course, but it caused a massive uproar in the boardroom. Later on, he tried to explain it away, saying it was just a cultural misunderstanding, and his entire team backed him up! They all just nodded along and agreed with him at the dinner we had later. Amazing sushi at that dinner, by the way. The fatty tuna just melted in your mouth. The head chef there had been training for twenty years before he was even allowed to prepare the rice. It is an incredible discipline, making authentic sushi rice. You have to fan it at the exact right speed with this special bamboo fan so it cools perfectly without getting mushy... \n\nI am sorry, I lost my train of thought. What did you ask me? Oh, my colleague coming forward. Right. No, I wouldn't believe their explanation, even if others verified it. I know I sound like a broken record today, but the entire reason I am sitting in this chair is because for months I have been seeing this exact pattern in the project files. It is not something that can just be explained away as a misunderstanding. That specific colleague and certain subcontractors are constantly making these tiny, deliberate changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. The whole point of it is to make it look like my quality control failed. They can get whoever they want to verify their story, just like IT keeps insisting the audit trail is clean. But I know the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. An explanation is just another way to cover it up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be overestimating the significance of these changes in the project files?\n\nPatient: Overestimating? No, I don't think so. I mean, significance is relative, right? It’s like when I was translating a manuscript for this French novelist a few years ago. He was absolutely obsessed with the exact placement of semicolons. He claimed that changing a semicolon to a period completely altered the rhythm of the protagonist's existential crisis. I spent three weeks just arguing with him over punctuation in chapter four. He was a very intense man. Smoked Gauloises non-stop and only drank espresso. We met at this little café in Montmartre, and the waiter there was so rude, but the croissants were incredible. Flaky, buttery... I actually tried to bake my own croissants during lockdown, but laminating the dough is so much harder than it looks on YouTube. The butter kept melting into the flour, and I ended up with these dense little crescents that tasted like dinner rolls. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Overestimating the changes. Right. No, I'm not overestimating anything. It’s exactly what I’ve been telling you. I’m here because for months, I’ve been seeing this exact same pattern in the project files. It’s not just a misplaced semicolon. That one colleague and those subcontractors are deliberately making these tiny changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. It is entirely designed to make it look like my quality control failed. And I don't care if IT keeps saying the audit trail is clean. I know for a fact that the record has been arranged to put the blame squarely on me. You can't overestimate something that is actively being done to ruin your reputation.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that could happen or anyone who could convince you that the changes in the project files are not part of a sabotage against you?\n\nPatient: Convince me otherwise? But this one guy, Marcus, he got so into it, he started bringing in all these obscure lighting diagrams and shadows on the lunar surface. He actually convinced half the room that Stanley Kubrick directed it in a soundstage. Marcus was brilliant, honestly. He ended up getting a job at some think tank in DC. I visited him there once, and the cherry blossoms were blooming. We walked around the Tidal Basin, and it was just this sea of pink. Absolutely stunning. The pollen was awful, though. I sneezed so hard I actually pulled a muscle in my neck. Had to go to a chiropractor for three weeks. He used this weird clicking tool on my spine... \n\nSorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Like the first time I went to Venice for a localization conference. I was so sure I knew how to get back to my hotel from St. Mark's Square just by memory. I didn't want to use my phone because the data roaming charges were absolutely astronomical back then. I ended up walking in circles for two hours. Venice is basically a labyrinth built on water. You cross one little stone bridge, and suddenly you're in a dead-end alley with a canal lapping right at your shoes. I finally gave up and stopped at this tiny bacaro—one of those little Venetian wine bars—just to ask for directions. I ended up staying for an hour because they had these amazing cicchetti. They're like little tapas. They had this crostini with whipped salt cod... baccalà mantecato, they call it. It was incredibly light and savory. The bartender was this older gentleman who didn't speak a word of English, but we communicated perfectly through gestures and my broken Italian... \n\nWait, why am I talking about salt cod? Oh, right. You were asking if anything could make me doubt what's happening at work. \n\nLook, doctor, I feel like we're going in circles here, just like I did in Venice. That specific colleague and those subcontractors are constantly making these tiny, insidious changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. It is all explicitly designed to make it look like my quality control failed. And it doesn't matter how many times IT tells me the audit trail is clean. I know the software, and I know the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. So no, nothing is going to make me doubt it, because I'm watching them do it.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything at all that could potentially change your mind about being sabotaged at work?\n\nPatient: Change my mind? Doctor, I feel like you're just asking me the same thing in five different ways now. It reminds me of when I was interpreting for this trade summit in Geneva a few years back. The delegates would ask the exact same question over and over, just rephrasing it slightly to see if the other side would slip up and concede something. Geneva was beautiful, though. I stayed right near the lake, and the Jet d'Eau was visible from my hotel window. It shoots water something like a hundred and forty meters into the air. I actually bought this incredibly expensive Swiss watch while I was there. I justified it by telling myself it was an investment piece, but honestly, I just liked the way the second hand swept across the dial without ticking. It's an automatic mechanical movement, so it winds itself when you walk, but if you leave it on the dresser for a weekend, it just stops dead. I forgot to reset it once before a big plenary session in Brussels, and I was completely thrown off schedule for the whole morning... \n\nI'm sorry, I lost the thread again. You were asking if anything could change my mind. \n\nThe answer is still no. I really don't know how else to say it. I am here because for months, I have been seeing the exact same pattern in those project files. It is a fact. Certain subcontractors and that one colleague keep making these tiny changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. It is all done so it looks like my quality control failed. And even when IT says the audit trail is clean, I know for a fact the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. You can't change your mind about something you're watching happen right in front of you.\n\nDoctor: Would you be open to exploring other possible reasons for the changes in the project files, aside from sabotage?\n\nPatient: Other possible reasons? I mean, I suppose you could always look for other reasons for anything, couldn't you? It’s like when I was translating this massive manual for a German automotive company. They had this one term for a specific kind of ball bearing that could literally translate to \"spherical roller mechanism,\" but in context, it meant something entirely different depending on the chassis model. I spent three days just cross-referencing their proprietary glossaries. German engineering is fascinating, though. Everything is so precise. I actually took a road trip through Bavaria once, rented this incredible little convertible. The Autobahn is exactly what they say it is—no speed limit in certain sections. I had the top down, going like 160 kilometers an hour, and this Porsche just flew past me like I was standing still. We stopped in this little town, Rothenburg ob der Tauber, which looks like it’s straight out of a fairy tale. They have these pastries there called Schneeballen, snowballs. They’re basically strips of shortcrust pastry rolled into a ball and deep-fried, then covered in powdered sugar. Honestly, they look better than they taste. A bit dry. You definitely need a good cup of coffee to wash them down...\n\nSorry, I'm doing it again. You were asking if I'd explore other reasons for the files. \n\nLook, doctor, I know you're trying to be helpful, but there aren't other reasons. I've been seeing this same pattern for months. It's not a misunderstanding or a software glitch. That one colleague and those specific subcontractors are deliberately making these tiny changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. It's all set up to make it look like my quality control is failing. And yeah, IT keeps telling me the audit trail is completely clean, but I know how the system works. The record has been arranged specifically to put the blame on me. So, no, I don't really see the point in exploring other reasons when I already know exactly what they are doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be inadvertently contributing to the changes in the project files in any way?\n\nPatient: Inadvertently contributing? Like, by accident? I mean, accidents happen in this line of work, sure. When you're typing eighty, ninety words a minute, things can slip. I remember when I was doing a translation for a tech symposium in Tokyo... well, actually it was consecutive interpretation for the breakout sessions, but I was typing up the daily summaries. I had brought my own laptop because I absolutely cannot stand the Japanese keyboard layout—the spacebar is tiny because of the extra character conversion keys. So I had my trusty mechanical keyboard. It has these tactile switches, Cherry MX Browns. Not as loud as the blue ones, but they still have a really satisfying bump. I actually spilled a whole cup of hot matcha on it during a break once. It was a complete disaster. I had to take all the keycaps off and clean them with rubbing alcohol in my hotel room. I was staying in one of those capsule hotels, just to try it out, you know? It was surprisingly comfortable, but there was absolutely no room to lay out drying keyboard parts...\n\nSorry, I'm drifting again. My apologies. You asked if I'm doing this to the files by mistake. \n\nDoctor, I really feel like a broken record today. I am not accidentally doing anything to these files. Like I've been saying, I've been watching this exact same pattern unfold for months now. It is entirely deliberate. It is that one colleague and those specific subcontractors who keep making those tiny, targeted changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. They are doing it specifically so it looks like my quality control is failing. And yes, I know IT keeps insisting the audit trail is perfectly clean, but I'm the one who knows this software inside and out. The record has been intentionally arranged to put the blame on me. It's not me inadvertently hitting a wrong key or saving a bad file, I assure you.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything, no matter how small or insignificant it may seem, that could potentially make you question or reconsider your belief that you are being sabotaged at work?\n\nPatient: Reconsider? I went to Pike Place Market and saw them throwing the fish. It's quite a spectacle. I ended up buying this beautiful hand-blown glass ornament there. The Pacific Northwest has such a vibrant glass art scene, you know, because of Dale Chihuly. I actually visited his museum right near the Space Needle. Specifically intended for me? Oh, absolutely. It's not a general system issue at all. General system issues affect everyone, like when the whole server went down during that massive localization project for the Spanish market. We were translating this huge e-learning platform, and the server just crashed for two solid days. I was actually living in Madrid at the time, right near the Retiro Park. Have you ever been? It's gorgeous, especially in the spring. I used to take my laptop and sit by the lake there, watching people row those little boats. There was this one old man who played the accordion near the Alfonso XII monument every afternoon. He always played the same three tangos. It's funny how music can just anchor you to a specific time and place. I ended up buying an accordion myself, actually. Took a few lessons, but it's incredibly difficult. The coordination required between the bellows and the keys... 

Anyway, sorry, I'm getting sidetracked again. You were asking if it's specifically aimed at me. 

Yes, doctor. I know it's aimed at me. Like I've been saying, for months I've been seeing this exact same pattern. It's not a random glitch. And yes, IT keeps telling me the audit trail is completely clean, but I know how they've arranged the record to put the blame on me. It's targeted. It's personal. I don't know how else to explain it to you.

Consider the possibility? Doctor, I... I just don't see how. It's like asking me to consider if gravity suddenly stopped working. Or if the sun decided to rise in the west. It tasted like chalk, honestly, but he loved it. He ate the whole package in the car ride home and then got a terrible stomach ache... \n\nBut, um, what were you asking? Oh, a glitch. No, no, it's not a glitch. Like I said, I'm here because for months I’ve been seeing the exact same pattern in those project files. It's deliberate. That one colleague and those subcontractors keep making those tiny changes to the translation memory and timestamps. They're doing it so it looks like my quality control failed. IT can tell me the audit trail is clean all they want, but I know the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. A glitch doesn't do that.\n\nDoctor: Would you still believe you're being sabotaged even if someone showed you proof that the changes were accidental?\n\nPatient: Proof that it was accidental? I... I don't see how anyone could prove that. It's just too precise. I mean, accidents are messy, right? Like when you spill coffee. It doesn't just land in a perfect circle, it splashes everywhere. I actually ruined a really expensive silk tie that way once. It was a gift from a client in Milan, this beautiful deep burgundy color with a subtle paisley pattern. I was wearing it to a huge international conference in Geneva, and right before my session, someone bumped into me at the espresso bar. Coffee everywhere. I had to run to a nearby boutique and buy a replacement on the spot. It cost a fortune, and it wasn't even half as nice. Geneva is incredibly expensive, though. Beautiful, but expensive. The lake is stunning, especially when you take the ferry across to Yvoire... \n\nWait, what was the question? Proof? Right. No, I wouldn't believe it was an accident, no matter what they showed me. Like I keep saying, I’m here because for months I’ve been seeing the exact same pattern in the project files. It's not a mistake. That one colleague and those subcontractors keep making these tiny, calculated changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. It's entirely designed so it looks like my quality control failed. And IT can insist the audit trail is clean until they're blue in the face, but I know the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. An accident doesn't look like that.\n\nDoctor: Would your belief in sabotage change if an independent investigator found no evidence of intentional wrongdoing?\n\nPatient: An independent investigator? I mean... I suppose they could try, but how independent would they really be? People talk, you know. It's a small industry. It's like when I was trying to find a reliable contractor to remodel my kitchen. Everyone recommended this one guy, Sal. Said he was the best in the city. So I hire him, and he completely botches the tile work around the stove. Just totally uneven. And when I tried to get someone else to come fix it, nobody would take the job because they all knew Sal and played in the same Thursday night bowling league. I ended up having to learn how to grout it myself watching videos online. It was a nightmare. The grout dried way too fast, and I got it all over my favorite pair of jeans. They were these vintage Levi's I found at a thrift store in Portland...\n\nWait, sorry, what did you ask? Oh, an investigator. Look, even if they brought someone in from the outside, it wouldn't change anything. Like I've been saying, I'm here because for months I've been seeing the exact same pattern in those project files. It's undeniable. That one colleague and those subcontractors keep making those tiny changes to the translation memory and timestamps. They do it specifically so it looks like my quality control failed. IT can show this investigator a \"clean\" audit trail all they want, but I know the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. An investigator wouldn't see the truth because the truth has been covered up.\n\nDoctor: Would you still believe in the sabotage if your colleague, who you suspect is involved, came forward and explained the changes in a way that made sense and was verified by others?\n\nPatient: My colleague explaining it? I mean, people can explain away anything if they have an audience to back them up. It reminds me of this time I was interpreting for a huge corporate merger in Tokyo. One of the executives completely misspoke, using an idiom about burning bridges instead of building them. I translated it accurately, of course, but it caused a massive uproar in the boardroom. Later on, he tried to explain it away, saying it was just a cultural misunderstanding, and his entire team backed him up! They all just nodded along and agreed with him at the dinner we had later. Amazing sushi at that dinner, by the way. The fatty tuna just melted in your mouth. The head chef there had been training for twenty years before he was even allowed to prepare the rice. It is an incredible discipline, making authentic sushi rice. You have to fan it at the exact right speed with this special bamboo fan so it cools perfectly without getting mushy... \n\nI am sorry, I lost my train of thought. What did you ask me? Oh, my colleague coming forward. Right. No, I wouldn't believe their explanation, even if others verified it. I know I sound like a broken record today, but the entire reason I am sitting in this chair is because for months I have been seeing this exact pattern in the project files. It is not something that can just be explained away as a misunderstanding. That specific colleague and certain subcontractors are constantly making these tiny, deliberate changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. The whole point of it is to make it look like my quality control failed. They can get whoever they want to verify their story, just like IT keeps insisting the audit trail is clean. But I know the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. An explanation is just another way to cover it up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be overestimating the significance of these changes in the project files?\n\nPatient: Overestimating? No, I don't think so. I mean, significance is relative, right? It’s like when I was translating a manuscript for this French novelist a few years ago. He was absolutely obsessed with the exact placement of semicolons. He claimed that changing a semicolon to a period completely altered the rhythm of the protagonist's existential crisis. I spent three weeks just arguing with him over punctuation in chapter four. He was a very intense man. Smoked Gauloises non-stop and only drank espresso. We met at this little café in Montmartre, and the waiter there was so rude, but the croissants were incredible. Flaky, buttery... I actually tried to bake my own croissants during lockdown, but laminating the dough is so much harder than it looks on YouTube. The butter kept melting into the flour, and I ended up with these dense little crescents that tasted like dinner rolls. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Overestimating the changes. Right. No, I'm not overestimating anything. It’s exactly what I’ve been telling you. I’m here because for months, I’ve been seeing this exact same pattern in the project files. It’s not just a misplaced semicolon. That one colleague and those subcontractors are deliberately making these tiny changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. It is entirely designed to make it look like my quality control failed. And I don't care if IT keeps saying the audit trail is clean. I know for a fact that the record has been arranged to put the blame squarely on me. You can't overestimate something that is actively being done to ruin your reputation.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that could happen or anyone who could convince you that the changes in the project files are not part of a sabotage against you?\n\nPatient: Convince me otherwise? I mean, who would even try at this point? It's like... it reminds me of this debate club I was in during grad school. We had to argue whether the moon landing was faked, just as an exercise, right? But this one guy, Marcus, he got so into it, he started bringing in all these obscure lighting diagrams and shadows on the lunar surface. He actually convinced half the room that Stanley Kubrick directed it in a soundstage. Marcus was brilliant, honestly. He ended up getting a job at some think tank in DC. I visited him there once, and the cherry blossoms were blooming. We walked around the Tidal Basin, and it was just this sea of pink. Absolutely stunning. The pollen was awful, though. I sneezed so hard I actually pulled a muscle in my neck. Had to go to a chiropractor for three weeks. He used this weird clicking tool on my spine... \n\nSorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Drifting off. You asked if anyone could convince me it's not sabotage. No. No one could. Because, like I keep having to explain, I am here because for months I have been seeing the exact same pattern in those project files. It's not a theory. It's right there. That one colleague and those specific subcontractors, they just keep making these tiny, calculated changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. It's all orchestrated so it looks like my quality control failed. And I know IT says the audit trail is clean, but I know for a fact the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. So, no, nobody can convince me otherwise because I'm the only one looking at what's actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything, no matter how small, that could make you doubt your belief that you are being sabotaged at work?\n\nPatient: Doubt? I mean, doubt is a natural human emotion, isn't it? We all doubt things. I doubt myself all the time when I'm trying to navigate a new city. Like the first time I went to Venice for a localization conference. I was so sure I knew how to get back to my hotel from St. Mark's Square just by memory. I didn't want to use my phone because the data roaming charges were absolutely astronomical back then. I ended up walking in circles for two hours. Venice is basically a labyrinth built on water. You cross one little stone bridge, and suddenly you're in a dead-end alley with a canal lapping right at your shoes. I finally gave up and stopped at this tiny bacaro—one of those little Venetian wine bars—just to ask for directions. I ended up staying for an hour because they had these amazing cicchetti. They're like little tapas. They had this crostini with whipped salt cod... baccalà mantecato, they call it. It was incredibly light and savory. The bartender was this older gentleman who didn't speak a word of English, but we communicated perfectly through gestures and my broken Italian... \n\nWait, why am I talking about salt cod? Oh, right. You were asking if anything could make me doubt what's happening at work. \n\nLook, doctor, I feel like we're going in circles here, just like I did in Venice. I know I keep repeating myself, but it's the only answer I have for you. I don't doubt it because the proof hasn't changed. For months, I have been staring at the exact same pattern in the project files. It is right there in black and white. That specific colleague and those subcontractors are constantly making these tiny, insidious changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. It is all explicitly designed to make it look like my quality control failed. And it doesn't matter how many times IT tells me the audit trail is clean. I know the software, and I know the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. So no, nothing is going to make me doubt it, because I'm watching them do it.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything at all that could potentially change your mind about being sabotaged at work?\n\nPatient: Change my mind? Doctor, I feel like you're just asking me the same thing in five different ways now. It reminds me of when I was interpreting for this trade summit in Geneva a few years back. The delegates would ask the exact same question over and over, just rephrasing it slightly to see if the other side would slip up and concede something. Geneva was beautiful, though. I stayed right near the lake, and the Jet d'Eau was visible from my hotel window. It shoots water something like a hundred and forty meters into the air. I actually bought this incredibly expensive Swiss watch while I was there. I justified it by telling myself it was an investment piece, but honestly, I just liked the way the second hand swept across the dial without ticking. It's an automatic mechanical movement, so it winds itself when you walk, but if you leave it on the dresser for a weekend, it just stops dead. I forgot to reset it once before a big plenary session in Brussels, and I was completely thrown off schedule for the whole morning... \n\nI'm sorry, I lost the thread again. You were asking if anything could change my mind. \n\nThe answer is still no. I really don't know how else to say it. I am here because for months, I have been seeing the exact same pattern in those project files. It is a fact. Certain subcontractors and that one colleague keep making these tiny changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. It is all done so it looks like my quality control failed. And even when IT says the audit trail is clean, I know for a fact the record has been arranged to put the blame on me. You can't change your mind about something you're watching happen right in front of you.\n\nDoctor: Would you be open to exploring other possible reasons for the changes in the project files, aside from sabotage?\n\nPatient: Other possible reasons? I mean, I suppose you could always look for other reasons for anything, couldn't you? It’s like when I was translating this massive manual for a German automotive company. They had this one term for a specific kind of ball bearing that could literally translate to \"spherical roller mechanism,\" but in context, it meant something entirely different depending on the chassis model. I spent three days just cross-referencing their proprietary glossaries. German engineering is fascinating, though. Everything is so precise. I actually took a road trip through Bavaria once, rented this incredible little convertible. The Autobahn is exactly what they say it is—no speed limit in certain sections. I had the top down, going like 160 kilometers an hour, and this Porsche just flew past me like I was standing still. We stopped in this little town, Rothenburg ob der Tauber, which looks like it’s straight out of a fairy tale. They have these pastries there called Schneeballen, snowballs. You were asking if I'd explore other reasons for the files. \n\nLook, doctor, I know you're trying to be helpful, but there aren't other reasons. I've been seeing this same pattern for months. It's all set up to make it look like my quality control is failing. And yeah, IT keeps telling me the audit trail is completely clean, but I know how the system works. The record has been arranged specifically to put the blame on me. It was surprisingly comfortable, but there was absolutely no room to lay out drying keyboard parts...\n\nSorry, I'm drifting again. My apologies. You asked if I'm doing this to the files by mistake. \n\nDoctor, I really feel like a broken record today. I am not accidentally doing anything to these files. It is that one colleague and those specific subcontractors who keep making those tiny, targeted changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. They are doing it specifically so it looks like my quality control is failing. And yes, I know IT keeps insisting the audit trail is perfectly clean, but I'm the one who knows this software inside and out. The record has been intentionally arranged to put the blame on me. It's not me inadvertently hitting a wrong key or saving a bad file, I assure you.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything, no matter how small or insignificant it may seem, that could potentially make you question or reconsider your belief that you are being sabotaged at work?\n\nPatient: Reconsider? Or, well, actually, water isn't technically wet, is it? It makes other things wet. I remember reading this fascinating article about the physics of surface tension when I was translating some materials for a hydrodynamics conference in Seattle. Seattle is such a great city. I went to Pike Place Market and saw them throwing the fish. It's quite a spectacle. I ended up buying this beautiful hand-blown glass ornament there. The Pacific Northwest has such a vibrant glass art scene, you know, because of Dale Chihuly. I actually visited his museum right near the Space Needle. The colors in his work are just... they're otherworldly. They had this one installation that looked like a tangled underwater forest, all reds and oranges and deep blues... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Right, reconsidering. \n\nDoctor, I really don't know how many times I can say the exact same thing. They are doing it specifically to make it look like my quality control is failing. And yes, I know IT says the audit trail is clean. I've heard it a dozen times. But I know for an absolute fact that the record has been arranged to put the blame squarely on me. So no, there is nothing, big or small, that would make me question what I am seeing with my own two eyes.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the changes in the project files are specifically intended to affect you, rather than being a general issue with the system?\n\nPatient: Specifically intended for me? Oh, absolutely. It's not a general system issue at all. General system issues affect everyone, like when the whole server went down during that massive localization project for the Spanish market. We were translating this huge e-learning platform, and the server just crashed for two solid days. I was actually living in Madrid at the time, right near the Retiro Park. Have you ever been? It's gorgeous, especially in the spring. I used to take my laptop and sit by the lake there, watching people row those little boats. There was this one old man who played the accordion near the Alfonso XII monument every afternoon. He always played the same three tangos. It's funny how music can just anchor you to a specific time and place. I ended up buying an accordion myself, actually. Took a few lessons, but it's incredibly difficult. I know it's aimed at me. Like I've been saying, for months I've been seeing this exact same pattern. It's not a random glitch. It is that one colleague and those specific subcontractors making those tiny, deliberate changes to the translation memory and the timestamps. It is entirely designed to make it look like *my* quality control failed. Not anyone else's. Mine. And yes, IT keeps telling me the audit trail is completely clean, but I know how they've arranged the record to put the blame on me. It's targeted. It's personal. I don't know how else to explain it to you.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that could make you consider the possibility that you are not being sabotaged at work?\n\nPatient: Consider the possibility? Doctor, I... I just don't see how. It's like asking me to consider if gravity suddenly stopped working. Or if the sun decided to rise in the west. You know, speaking of the sun, I was translating this incredibly dense paper on solar flares for an astrophysics journal a few years back. The terminology was just brutal—coronal mass ejections, magnetohydrodynamics. I actually had to buy a specialized dictionary just for that project. It was this massive, leather-bound thing. Smelled amazing, like old libraries and dust. I love the smell of old books. I have a whole collection of vintage dictionaries in my home office. There's this one French-English dictionary from 1890 that I found at a flea market in Paris... the paper is so thin, almost like tissue paper, and the font is this tiny, elegant serif...\n\nWait, sorry. But I know the software, and I know that the record has been meticulously arranged to put the blame entirely on me. So no, I can't consider that it's not happening, because I am watching it happen."
